THE GEORGE WASHINGTON UNIVERSITY Washington, D.C. 

LAW SCHOOL FACULTY AN ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

A great law school acquires its position by virtue of the quality of its students, the competence of its Faculty, and the adequacy of its educational and physical facilities. At this time when a new 

Law Alumni Directory is being issued it is fitting to acknowledge the invaluable service rendered the Law School by its Faculty throughout its growth and development-as the Columbian College of Law from 1865 until 1904, and since then, as the George Wash ington University Law School. 

The George Washington University (name change, 1904), chartered by Congress in 1821 as Columbian College, is the only nonsectarian university in the District of Columbia. It has provided a favorable environment for an impartial and independent approach to legal education. The many distinguished gentlemen who have been associated with the Law School as members of the Faculty, have, through their tutorial ability and professional achievements, moved the George Washington University Law

School into the forefront of American legal education. 

The following Faculty Roll is submitted to you with the expectation that you will wish to join in sharing this acknowledgment to these devoted scholars.

O. S. COLCLOUGH Dean 

January 2, 1953

FOREWORD 

It is a pleasure to convey my best wishes to the alumni of the George Washington University Law School with the issuance of the 1953 Law Alumni DirectORY. During the twenty-six years I have served as President of the University, I have had the pleasure of presenting diplomas to most of you. Since your graduation I have had the privilege of meeting many of you here in Washington and in my travels about the country. To all of you I extend the greetings of the Law School, the Board of Trustees, and myself. 

I wish also to thank those of you who are actively participating in the activities of the University Alumni Clubs and the Law Association. Through your interest and support  will continue to meet its obligations to the community with courage, imagination, and skill. 1 1 1 1 1 1 1 

CLOYD HECK MARVIN 

President of the University 910037 1 1 1 1 1 [v]

THE GEORGE WASHINGTON UNIVERSITY LAW SCHOOL  Law School, the oldest Law School in the 

District of Columbia, has been in continuous session since 1865. The present building at 720 20th Street, N. W., was opened in 1925. It is named for 

Admiral Stockton during whose term as President of the University plans for the building were first developed. 

In 1865 there were but two instructors. Today there are forty-one on the active faculty and teaching staff, of whom eighteen devote full time to the University.

Academic standards have always been of paramount concern. In 1900 The 

George Washington University Law School joined with other leading Universities in the formation of the Association of American Law Schools. The Law School is thus a charter member of this organization dedicated to improving the standards of legal education. Today a Bachelor's degree is required for admission to the Law School. The number of courses in the curriculum has been continually increased in order to keep abreast of modern trends and developments. During the past few years a complete study of the curriculum has been made, resulting in numerous improvements. Academic standards are identical for the Morning and 

Evening Divisions. 

Extra-curricular activities available to the law student include: 

1. The Student Bar Association—an organization of all law students. 

2. Amicus Curiae-the Law School newspaper. 

3. The George Washington Law Review—a publication which is devoted to legal problems in the public law field. Incidentally, it is believed to be the only Law Review which devotes its full effort to this field. 

4. The Case Club-A student organization offering experience in appellate practice.

In 1926, a chapter of the Order of the Coif, the national honor society, was established at this Law School. 

Another recent development is the increasing emphasis upon graduate and continuing legal education. These programs make provision for three types of post graduate work: (1) programs leading to advanced degrees (LL.M., M.C.L., S.J.D.); (2) a program of advanced courses for lawyers not seeking advanced degrees; (3) a program of lectures for lawyers and other professional people treating timely topics. This lecture program is entirely independent of the regular curriculum and customary recitation and examination requirements. 

The all-time peak enrollment in the Law School was 1700 during the Academic Year 1949-50 when the Veteran's Program placed its strain upon educational facilities everywhere. Today the enrollment is approximately 1000, of which two-thirds are in the Evening Division. Students come from the fortyeight states, the three territories, and thirty foreign countries. 

The George Washington University Law School is not content to maintain the status quo. One of the major improvements sought is the construction of new and more adequate physical facilities culminating in the establishment of a Law Center. In the Law Center all the constituent branches of the profession, in- [ix]

cluding judges, lawyers of all specialties, teachers, and both graduate and undergraduate law students, will join together to work on problems affecting not only the legal profession, but the country generally. The Law Center, with its facilities for research and study, can be expected to make valuable contributions to the improvement of substantive and procedural law and offer attractive inducements for research in public law. 

The Law School, which will be the axis around which the Center operates, will gain increased scope and effectiveness in its functions. An atmosphere of professional activity and achievement will pervade the Law Center bringing forth the best qualities of each student. With the development of this project, The 

George Washington University Law School will have reached the goal of a national institution with unlimited capacity for professional progress. [ x ]

PREFACE The LAW ALUMNI DIRECTORY contains information concerning holders of law degrees conferred by the Law School of  from 1866 to November 1952 inclusive. 

To conserve space and minimize typographical error, a compressed style with as little punctuation as is consistent with clarity has been adopted for the DIRECTORY, and a generous use has been made of abbreviations. Spacing has been employed to set off the groups of information. It is hoped that with the aid of the following explanatory paragraphs and lists of symbols and abbreviations the individual entries in the DIRECTORY will be found readily intelligible. 

The DIRECTORY comprises three lists: geographical, alphabetical, and class. 

GEOGRAPHICAL LIST 

This list contains the names of all living alumni of the Law School of The George Washington University with the exception of those alumni whose addresses are unknown at this time. The list is arranged alphabetically by state and city, and within each city the names are alphabetical. Degrees from the Law 

School are given first, followed by other degrees from The George Washington University, and, when available, degrees from other institutions. After the degrees the person's field of specialization in law is listed followed by his position held and name of firm, government agency or other employer as far as this information was available. The listing of the field of specialization is with the approval of the Standing Committee on Law Lists of the American Bar Association. Those alumni who are outside of the field of law have their position and employer listed. For those alumni who are overseas and whose addresses are 

Army Post Office or Fleet Post Office, there is a separate listing at the end of the Geographical List. The business address is used where available and, if the residence address is used, it is preceded by the abbreviation Res. The names of the alumni as given are the names as carried by the person at present; for cross reference in the case of name changes, see the Alphabetical List. When two or more institutions of the same name exist, the state or city in which the one referred to is situated is given in brackets unless the context makes clear which one is meant. The name of a foreign institution is followed in brackets by the name of the country in which it is situated. 

New information was incorporated as much as possible, up to December 1952. 

ALPHABETICAL LIST 

This list contains the names of all living alumni of the Law School, followed by their present location (city and state). When the location of an alumnus is unknown, the term "Address Unknown" appears after his name. The names of [xi]

alumni are here listed as they were at the time of graduation, and in the case of a change of name a cross reference to the present name is given. 

CLASS LIST 

This list contains all alumni living and deceased of the Law School. An asterisk (*) precedes the names of deceased alumni. The names of recipients of honorary degrees are followed by (h). The names of alumni who received more than one degree in the Law School appear only once-under the year that the first degree was attained.

In compiling the DIRECTORY every effort has been made to secure as complete and accurate information as possible. On three separate occasions questionnaires asking for information for the DIRECTORY were issued to all alumni on the mailing list. In addition, there was sent to all alumni a list of the graduates whose addresses were unknown, with a request for assistance in locating them. 

A compilation of this sort, where accuracy depends upon the collaboration of a great many persons, is subject to errors and omissions. Corrections and additions sent by the users of this volume will be appreciatively received. The 

DIRECTORY staff has made every effort to ensure the correctness of all information, and has consulted a wide variety of sources wherever it was without recent information sent by an alumnus personally. Direct communication from an alumnus, however, has been assumed to be accurate. 

The cooperation of the alumni in this undertaking has been most gratifying, and the University is greatly indebted to the many persons who have graciously and promptly supplied and verified information for the DIRECTORY. 

MARY DOW
